## Page 1


ON CANONICITY FOR INTEGRAL MODELS OF SHIMURA VARIETIES WITH
HYPERSPECIAL LEVEL
KEERTHI MADAPUSI AND ALEX YOUCIS
Abstract. We give a new definition—and in some cases, a new construction—of integral canonical models of
Shimura varieties that uses the notion of an aperture appearing in work of Gardner–Madapusi on some conjectures
of Drinfeld. This applies to Shimura varieties of pre-abelian type at odd primes of hyperspecial level, recovering and
extending previous work of Kisin, Kim–Madapusi and Imai–Kato–Youcis, but also to exceptional Shimura varieties
for large enough primes. The characterization in the exceptional case is a priori different from the one recently
shown by Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman, and recovers many of their results, such as the existence of prime-to-p Hecke
operators, the non-emptiness of the µ-ordinary stratum and the theory of the canonical lift.
In fact, we give a
uniform proof of the non-emptiness of all possible Newton strata, and of the non-emptiness of Ekedahl–Oort strata
and central leaves as well. An important ingredient in the proofs is a generalization of Tate’s full faithfulness theorem
for p-divisible groups to the context of apertures. This leads to a mapping property for the integral canonical model
that characterizes maps into it from all normal, flat and excellent schemes over Z(p).

1. Introduction
The purpose of this article is to lay the groundwork for applying the results of [GM24] to global questions. In
doing so, we give a p-adic characterization of integral canonical models of Shimura varieties at unramified primes,
including for those of exceptional type considered by Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman [BST25a]. This characterization
is a priori different from the ℓ-adic ones given in op. cit. and others, and is closely related to those introduced
by Pappas [Pap23] and Imai–Kato–Youcis [IKY23]. For Shimura varieties of abelian type, our method involves a
new interpretation of Kisin’s twisting construction, and we further extend it to give models for Shimura data of
pre-abelian type.1
1.1. Definition and characterization. The key observation animating this paper is that apertures, the mixed
characteristic analogues of the local shtukas of Genestier–Lafforgue, introduced by Drinfeld [Dri24b] and studied
in [GM24], have very strong connections with the integral theory of Shimura varieties, and in fact characterize the
1Pre-abelian Shimura data have a simple type-theoretic characterization: the adjoint datum is of type A, B, C, DR, or DH. The
first non-trivial example of one that is not of abelian type is a type DH Shimura datum with simply connected derived subgroup—see
Example 7.7.3.
1

made in [IKY23], where it is shown that the prismatic realization on an integral model of a Shimura variety of
abelian type is sufficient to pin down the model at finite level, without considering the whole prime-to-p Hecke
tower.
Our primary aim here is to situate this result in what we believe to be the right context, and to substantially
broaden the kinds of Shimura data it applies to by exploiting the geometry made available by the theory in [GM24].

In particular, the ICM SK is unique up to unique isomorphism, and is functorial in the unramified tuple (G, X, X, K).
Remark 1.1.5. The proof of the theorem is based on an argument of Pappas [Pap23, Theorem 7.1.7]. His use of
Tate’s full faithfulness theorem for p-divisible groups is replaced with that of a version for apertures; see §1.5 below.
A closely related argument also appears in [IKY23, Theorem 3.13].
Remark 1.1.6. The mapping property in Theorem A was essentially established for the natural integral models of

Remark 1.1.7 (Mapping property for Pappas–Rapoport models). The method of proof of Theorem A combined
with a fully faithfulness result of Pappas–Rapoport [PR24] establishes a mapping property for integral models at
parahoric levels satisfying the axioms from loc. cit.; see Theorem 8.7.17.
For p > 3, we have a simpler mapping property for ICMs with respect to smooth OE,(v)-schemes.
Theorem B (Pointwise mapping property, Proposition 8.3.2). Suppose that p > 3, that SK is an ICM for ShK,
and that X is a smooth OE,(v)-scheme with generic fiber X. Then a map f : X →ShK, extends to a map X →SK if
and only if, for all finite extensions F/Ev and all x ∈X(OF ) the local system EtK,p|x is crystalline. In particular,
if SK is proper over OE,(v), then it is a N´eron model for ShK as in [BLR90, Definition 1]: For any smooth
OE,(v)-scheme S, every map Sη →ShK of E-schemes extends to a map S →SK.
In terms of existence of such models, we have:
Theorem C (Pre-abelian-type canonical models, Theorem 7.7.6). Suppose that one of the following conditions
holds:
(1) (G, X) is of abelian type;
(2) p > 2 and (G, X) is of pre-abelian type.
Then, for Kp sufficiently small, ShK admits an ICM over OE,(v). In fact, in case (1), the models constructed by
Kisin [Kis10] (and Kim–Madapusi [KM16] for p = 2) are ICMs as defined here.
We also have the following version of a recent theorem of Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman [BST25a].
Theorem D (Exceptional integral canonical models, §7.8). For an unramified Shimura tuple (G, G, X, K), and all
p sufficiently large, an ICM SK exists over OE,(v) for any v|p.
Remark 1.1.8. Given Theorem C, the content of Theorem D is of course that it applies to the so-called exceptional
Shimura data that are not of pre-abelian type and hence cannot be related to the moduli of abelian varieties in any
reasonably direct way. As in [BST25a], the models here are obtained from a spreading out procedure, which leads
to the lack of control on prime p.
See §1.3 below for a more detailed comparison with existing results.
Remark 1.1.9 (Yet another canonical model?). The experienced reader may be wondering at this point if the
world really needs yet another notion of integral canonical models for Shimura varieties. The arguments we would
proffer in favor of the definition here—which we believe to be the correct one—are as follows:
(1) First, of course, one has the quite pleasing mapping property from Theorem A.
(2) Accounting systematically for the prime-to-p realizations, one can deduce various non-trivial properties for
such models using only their defining features, without invoking any moduli interpretation.
(3) Finally, in forthcoming work of the first author with Si Ying Lee, one will find that various results about inte-
gral models of abelian type (Rapoport–Zink uniformization, existence of CM lifts, Igusa stacks, Langlands–
Rapoport type point counting formulas, etc.), for which the use of abelian varieties has hitherto seemed
essential, are in fact properties of limpid ICMs as defined in the next subsection.

1.2. Limpid ICMs and their properties. To present our further results, we will need a slightly enhanced
definition of an ICM.
Remark 1.2.1 (Prime-to-p ´etale realizations). For ℓ̸= p, we also have Kc
ℓ-local systems EtK,ℓover ShK, with
Kc
ℓ⊂Gc(Qℓ) a compact open subgroup given by the image of K under the projection G(Af) →Gc(Qℓ).
Definition 1.2.2 (Limpid integral canonical models). An ICM SK for ShK over OE,(v) is limpid if, for all ℓ̸= p,
the Kc
ℓ-local system EtK,ℓextends over SK.4
Remark 1.2.3 (Pre-abelian ICMs are limpid). In the context of Theorem C, ICMs are in fact limpid. This follows
from our proof, which uses the three theorems of this subsection—theorems that do not use any sort of moduli
interpretation—to reduce to the essentially standard fact that the moduli of principally polarized abelian schemes
with appropriate level structure are limpid ICMs for Siegel modular varieties.
Remark 1.2.4 (Exceptional ICMs are limpid). The ICMs appearing in Theorem D are also limpid for large enough
p: This follows from work of Klevdal–Patrikis [KP25].
Theorem E (Integral canonical models from reduction of structure group, §7.2). Suppose that we have a map
(G, G, X, K) →(G♯, G♯, X♯, K♯) of unramified Shimura tuples with G →G♯a closed immersion of reductive group
schemes. If ShK♯admits an ICM over OE♯,(v♯) (for the place v♯| p of the reflex field E♯⊂E lying under v), then
so does ShK. Moreover, if the ICM for ShK♯is limpid, then so is the one for ShK.
Remark 1.2.5. Theorem E is already known by work of Pappas–Rapoport and Daniels–van Hoften–Kim–Zhang
in the context of Pappas–Rapoport canonical models; see [DvHKZ25, Theorem 4.1.8].
Next, we have two results concerning the behavior of limpid ICMs under central modifications.
Setup 1.2.6. We will fix a map of unramified Shimura tuples (G1, G1, X1, K1) →(G2, G2, X2, K2) with reflex fields
E2 ⊂E1 such that G1 →G2 is surjective with central kernel. We will also fix a place v1|p of E1 and let v2|p be the
place of E2 under it.
First, we have the following ascent statement. which implies that constructing ICMs for adjoint Shimura data
is sufficient to construct them for all Shimura data:
Theorem F (Ascent of ICMs along central isogenies, Theorem 7.5.4). Suppose that p > 2 and that G2 = Gc
2. If
ShK2 admits a limpid ICM SK2 over OE2,(v2), then ShK1 admits a limpid ICM SK1 over OE1,(v1), and the map
SK1 →SK2 is finite ´etale.
Remark 1.2.7. There is an obvious way to construct an integral model SK2 for ShK2 from an integral model SK1
for ShK1: One can take the normalization of SK1 in ShK2. The issue is to show that this preserves the property of
being a limpid ICM. The heart of the matter is to see that SK1 is finite ´etale over SK2. We prove this by showing
it holds for the restriction to the µ-ordinary locus and using purity. Over the µ-ordinary locus, we need the theory
of the canonical lift; see Theorem K below. The fact that the canonical lift is CM makes use of the prime-to-p
realizations, and so requires the additional limpidity hypothesis.
Remark 1.2.8. The idea of using the µ-ordinary locus and canonical lifts to prove something like an ascent theorem
shows up in a different form in work of Vasiu [Vas01, §4.9].
Remark 1.2.9 (Sufficiency of adjoint data). Theorem F tells us that, when p > 2, in order to construct limpid ICMs
for tuples with underlying data (G, G, X), it suffices to do so for tuples with adjoint Shimura data (Gad, Gad, Xad).
Next, we have an abstract form of the twisting construction from [Kis17], which uses the modified Weil restriction
given in Definition 7.6.14 below.

Theorem G (Descent of ICMs along central isogenies, Theorem 7.6.25). Suppose that G = Gc and that the
following additional condition holds for every totally real number field F in which p is unramified: All unramified
Shimura tuples with underlying unramified Shimura datum Res′

and Z(Gder)(Ap

sufficiently small, ShK2 admits a limpid ICM SK2 over OE2,(v2), and the map SK1 →SK2 is finite ´etale.
Remark 1.2.10 (Role of the modified Weil restriction condition). The role of the condition is to overcome the
fact that, in the generality that we work with here, we do not have a good intrinsic notion of ‘F-linear isogenies’
between points on the Shimura variety.
In the context of Shimura varieties of Hodge type, such isogenies, defined through the F-linearization of the
isogeny category of abelian varieties, are an important actor in Kisin’s twisting construction. This is replaced here
by a systematic use of the ICMs for the modified Weil restrictions (in fact from the actual Weil restrictions). The
condition of being of Hodge type is preserved under the modified Weil restriction construction, and so we obtain a
different perspective on the construction of Shimura varieties of abelian type from those of Hodge type. More than
anything else, this is a proof of concept that one can replace arguments using isogenies between abelian varieties
with ones involving structural properties of ICMs.
1.3. Comparison with existing results.
Remark 1.3.1 (Comparison with existing notions of canonicity). Our notion of canonicity is different from those
appearing in [Moo98], [Kis10] or [BST25a], which use ℓ-adic realizations for ℓ̸= p to characterize the model.
Also, the characterizations appearing in the first two of these, are not of models at any finite level, but of the
whole prime-to-p Hecke tower. As such, they are slightly tricky to formulate and check. Also, our definition of
ICMs is intrinsic to an integral model, with the mapping property arising as a consequence, while the other three
require some external input: consideration of the full prime-to-p Hecke tower, or the existence of a sufficiently nice
compactification.
The notion of an ICM used here was essentially introduced by Imai–Kato–Youcis [IKY23], and this in turn has
its antecedents in the work of Pappas [Pap23] and Pappas–Rapoport [PR24]. The important observation that this
should give a characterization at finite level is found in [IKY23].
Remark 1.3.2 (Comparison with the work of Kisin). The first general construction of integral canonical models
of abelian type is due to Kisin [Kis10]; see also the earlier work of Vasiu [Vas99; Vas01]. For Shimura varieties of
Hodge type, what one will find in this paper is essentially a streamlined version of the arguments in Kisin’s work,
making use of the geometry of the stacks BTGc,-µv
n
to compress some of the p-adic Hodge theory used there.
To construct Shimura varieties of abelian type, Kisin introduces his twisting construction, which ultimately uses
the global moduli interpretation of Siegel-type Shimura varieties in terms of abelian varieties. We actually give a
different approach to twisting here. The main new observation here is that one does not need a moduli description:
One can formulate the construction in a way that is valid for all maps between Shimura data where the underlying
map of groups is a central cover. We also make the additional new observation that the theory of the canonical
lift for µ-ordinary points suffices to also lift ICMs along central covers, at least when p > 2. This leads to our
construction of ICMs of pre-abelian type.
Remark 1.3.3 (Comparison with Imai–Kato–Youcis). That the models constructed by Kisin are ICMs in the
sense used here is originally due to Imai–Kato–Youcis [IKY23]. The proof we give here is different from that in
[IKY23] and works also when p = 2 where the models were constructed by Kim–Madapusi. But we do make use of
intermediate results from [IKY23; IKY24; IKY25].
Remark 1.3.4 (Comparison with Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman). The idea that one should be able to provably
characterize integral canonical models of all Shimura varieties, including those not of abelian type, first appears
in the already mentioned recent work of Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman. Their results extend beyond the ambit of
Shimura varieties, and apply to integral models of non-minuscule variations of Hodge structure. This is in some
sense the main philosophical difference between these works: Here, we make full use of the minusculeness (though
in the sense of G-bundles, not just vector bundles) of the family of realizations over a Shimura variety, for instance,

in the form of the full faithfulness result Theorem L. But these methods say nothing about what happens beyond
the minuscule regime.
The ICMs here are also canonical in the sense of [BST25a] for p large enough, and our proof of canonicity is
independent from theirs, though it uses essentially the same inputs. Moreover, the lower bound on the prime p is
a priori smaller than the one in op. cit.: for instance, it depends only on the underlying adjoint Shimura datum.
1.4. Applications. Our first application is essentially an algebraization result for apertures.
Theorem H (Surjectivity of the syntomic realization, Theorem 8.1.10). Suppose that ShK admits an ICM SK
over OE,(v) and that one of the following conditions holds:
(1) The Shimura datum (G, X) is of pre-abelian type.
(2) The prime p is sufficiently large (as in Theorem D).
Then, for all 1 ⩽n ⩽∞, the map
c
SK →BTGc,-µv
n
is surjective, and it is smooth if n < ∞.
Remark 1.4.1. The proof of Theorem H shows that the surjectivity holds when (G, G, X, K) satisfies the de Jong
extension property given in Definition 8.1.3 below. The cases listed in Theorem H are the ones where we can
verify this extension property; but we expect that it is valid for all ICMs.
Remark 1.4.2. The surjectivity assertion above is already known for Shimura varieties of PEL type by work
of Viehmann–Wedhorn [VW13]. Their proof amounts to first establishing the non-emptiness of Newton strata,
followed by an argument via isogenies. A similar argument works for Shimura varieties for Hodge and perhaps even
abelian type, but one has to work much harder by appealing to the non-emptiness results of [Lee18] or [KMS22],
as well as to some deeper results from [Kis17] on p-isogenies between mod-p points of the Shimura variety.
In contrast, the proof given here is geometric, works uniformly for all known ICMs, and requires no a priori
knowledge of non-emptiness statements or the existence of isogenies. In fact, it can be used to deduce such results.
Theorem I (Non-emptiness of Ekedahl–Oort strata, §7.4 and §8.2). Suppose that SK is an ICM. Then there exists
a smooth map of stacks
SK ⊗k(v) →Gc- zip-µv.
Here, the right-hand side is the stack of Gc-zips of type -µv defined in [PWZ15], and admits a canonical open and
dense stratum known as the µ-ordinary stratum. Moreover:
(1) The µ-ordinary locus of SK ⊗k(v) (which is the pre-image of the µ-ordinary stratum) is dense.
(2) If SK satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem H, then this map is also surjective: That is, all Ekedahl–Oort
strata of the special fiber are non-empty.
Remark 1.4.3. The density of the µ-ordinary locus is shown in [KMS22] for most Shimura varieties of Hodge type
with parahoric level at p, extending results of Wortmann [Wor13]. It is also known in the exceptional case if p is
sufficiently large by work of Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman [BST25a, §8].
Remark 1.4.4. The non-emptiness of Ekedahl–Oort strata for Shimura varieties of PEL type is due to Viehmann–
Wedhorn [VW13], and is also known in the abelian type case by work of Shen–Zhang [SZ22] (though also see earlier
work of Wortmann [Wor13] and Zhang [Zha18]). The proof of Shen–Zhang once again uses deep results of Kisin
from [Kis17]. On the other hand, our proof is essentially an immediate consequence of Theorem H, and the fact
(shown in [GM24]) that there is a natural smooth surjective map BTGc,-µv
1
⊗Fp →Gc- zip-µv.
There is another stratification where we can show non-emptiness for an abstract ICM (i.e., without requiring an
assumption as in Remark 1.4.1): the Newton stratification using the Kottwitz set B(Gc, {-µv}) defined in [Kot85]
and studied in [RR96].
Theorem J (Non-emptiness of Newton strata, Theorem 8.2.2). Let SK be an ICM for ShK over OE,(v). Then the
natural map SK →B(Gc, {-µv}) is surjective.

Next, we have applications to the µ-ordinary locus.
Theorem K (Canonical CM lifts for µ-ordinary points, Theorems 7.4.5, 7.4.17). Suppose that SK is an ICM for
ShK and that x ∈SK(κ) is a closed point in the µ-ordinary locus where κ is a finite field. Let bUx be the formal
completion of SK at x. Then:
(1) There exists a canonical tower
bUx = bUx,n →bUx,n−1 →· · · →bUx,0 = Spf W(κ)
of formal schemes over W(κ), where, bUx,i is a formal p-divisible group over bUx,i−1 for i > 0.
(2) Suppose that SK is limpid. Then the identity section5 xcan ∈bUx(W(κ)), the canonical lifting, is a CM
point of SK.
Remark 1.4.5. Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman also show the existence of the CM canonical lift for their integral
models [BST25a, §8]. Unlike their proof, ours does not use a priori knowledge of the ℓ-independence of the conjugacy
classes of Frobenius elements acting on the ´etale realizations (see Klevdal–Patrikis [KP25]): We only use the defining
properties of ICMs, and deduce this ℓ-independence from the existence of the canonical CM lift. The key point is
to exhibit a lift of Frobenius as a quasi-isogeny, which relies ultimately on the stack-theoretic methods used here.
Remark 1.4.6 (Integral canonical models for subhyperspecial levels). Finally, we mention that the results here
can be combined with work of Takaya [Tak25] to show the existence of integral canonical models in the sense of
Pappas–Rapoport when the level at p is parahoric and contained in a hyperspecial subgroup; see Proposition 8.7.13.
Remark 1.4.7 (Expected applications). We expect the results and methods here to be applied (among other
questions) to:
• The problem of defining p-Hecke correspondences and Igusa stacks for limpid ICMs, as well as counting
their mod-p points;
• An extension of the Langlands–Kottwitz–Scholze method;
• The understanding of central leaves;
• The study of special cycles on Shimura varieties.
In addition, in ongoing work, joint with Teruhisa Koshikawa and Kentaro Inoue, we will also extend these methods
to the logarithmic context and define integral canonical models for toroidal compactifications of Shimura varieties.
1.5. Tate full faithfulness. Finally, a key ingredient in our proofs of the canonicity results, and especially for
establishing the mapping property in Theorem A, is a generalization of Tate’s full faithfulness theorem for the Tate
module of p-divisible groups, and so is of independent interest.
This result holds in the following setting: G will be a smooth affine group scheme over Zp and µ will be a
cocharacter of G defined over an unramified ring of integers ˆO. We will assume that µ is 1-bounded in the sense
of Lau [Lau21, Definition 6.3.1] (when G is reductive, this is the same as being minuscule). Then we have the
associated formal algebraic stacks BTG,µ
n
constructed in [GM24, §9], and their inverse limit BTG,µ
∞. We also have
the ´etale realization functor from BTG,µ
∞
to G(Zp)-local systems on the generic fiber, generalizing the Tate module
of a p-divisible group.
Theorem L (Tate full faithfulness, Theorem 4.1.5). If X is a normal flat formal scheme over ˆO, the ´etale realization
functor from BTG,µ
∞(X) to G(Zp)-local systems over Xη is fully faithful.
The proof proceeds—as in Tate’s original proof—by reduction to the case of a discrete valuation ring. This
reduction requires algebraizing BTG,µ
n
to an algebraic stack over O with affine diagonal; see Theorem 4.1.3.

1.7. Notation and conventions.
• We follow the notational conventions of [GM24, §2] for (∞-)category theory and related notions.
• We will also follow the definitions and conventions of [GM24] for graded and filtered objects; see in particular
§4.1–4.3 of op. cit.
• As a specific instance, for a ring A and ideal I, we write Fil•
IA for the I-adic filtration. The trivial filtration
is FiltrivA
defn
= Fil0A. If I is invertible, we write Fil•
I,±A for the two-sided filtration with Filk
I,±A = Ik for
all k ∈Z.
• If V is a Gm-equivariant complex over some ring R, then we will write V i ⊂V for the direct summand
on which the Gm-action is via z 7→z−i: This is the i-th weight space for the action. This applies in
particular if µ : Gm →G is a cocharacter of a smooth affine group scheme G over R, where we take V to
be Lie(G) equipped with the adjoint action of Gm via µ.
• If R is a ring (or a scheme), we denote by bRI the completion along an ideal (sheaf) I. If I is clear from
context (it will usually be the ideal generated by p), we will consistently suppress it from the notation.
• We follow standard terminology and notation concerning Tannakian theory (e.g., Λ⊗); see, for example,
[IKY24, Appendix A] for a quick summary.
• For a ringed topos X, we write Vect(X) for the category of vector bundles and Perf(X) for the ∞-category
of perfect complexes.
• For a p-adic formal stack X, we denote by Xcrys the absolute crystalline site of X.
• For any groupoid G, and any algebraic space X, we will write G for the ´etale sheafification over X of the
constant groupoid associated with G.
• We will use a subscript η to denote ‘generic fiber’ in the following contexts:
(1) If X is an algebraic stack over a discrete valuation ring, then Xη will be the usual generic fiber over its
fraction field.
(2) If X is a formal algebraic stack over a p-complete discrete valuation ring O, then Xη will denote the
corresponding adic stack over Spa(O[1/p], O); see [OS25, §3-4].
• If (F, F +) is an affinoid field, and X is an algebraic stack over a F, we will write Xan for the associated
adic stack X ×Spec(F ) Spa(F, F +) over (F, F +); see [Hub94, §4].
2. Prismatic F-gauges
2.1. The stacks of Bhatt–Lurie and Drinfeld. Canonically associated with any derived p-adic formal scheme
X are three derived p-adic formal stacks6 X∆, XN, and Xsyn (shortened to R∆, RN, and Rsyn when X = Spf(R)), the
prismatization, filtered prismatization and syntomification, respectively. We will review what we’ll need to
know of these cohomological stacks for this paper. The reader can find more details in [Dri24a], [Bha], [GM24, §6]
and [MM25, §3]. The first two named sources explain how to construct these stacks as classical formal prestacks,
and the third extends the constructions—following Bhatt–Lurie [BL22]—to the animated framework.
Remark 2.1.1 (Prisms and the prismatization). The prismatization X∆parameterizes Cartier–Witt divisors on
X. These are related to the prismatic site for X. Indeed, suppose that we have an object (A, I, Spf(A) →X) in the
absolute prismatic site for X. Here A is endowed with the (p, I)-adic topology, and A = A/I. Then, as in [BL22,
Construction 3.10], we find a canonical map ι(A,I) : Spf A →X∆taking a map Spec(B) →Spf(A), where B is a
p-nilpotent ring, to the Cartier–Witt divisor I ⊗A W(B) →W(B) where A →W(B) is the unique δ-map lifting
A →B, and where we equip this Cartier–Witt divisor with structure map
Spec(W(B)/L(I ⊗A W(B))) →Spf(A) →X.
Remark 2.1.2 (Relationship between the stacks). The stack XN is a filtered formal stack, meaning that it is fibered
naturally over the formal Artin stack A1/Gm parameterizing line bundles L over p-complete rings C equipped with
6This means that they are ´etale (and in fact fpqc) sheaves on p-nilpotent derived affine schemes, i.e. derived schemes of the form
Spec A where A is a p-nilpotent animated commutative ring.

a cosection L →C. The pre-image of the open point
Spf Zp ≃Spf Zp × Gm/Gm ⊂Spf Zp × A1/Gm
is canonically isomorphic to X∆, and is called the de Rham locus of XN and its inclusion is denoted jdR : X∆→XN.
There is also another open immersion jHT : X∆→XN called the Hodge–Tate locus which is physically disjoint
from the de Rham locus. The stack Xsyn is the coequalizer of these two open immersions and is therefore equipped
with a canonical open immersion j∆: X∆→Xsyn.
Remark 2.1.3 (Nygaard filtered absolute prismatic cohomology). Bhatt–Lurie show that the values of these
stacks on quasiregular semiperfectoid (qrsp) inputs can be described in terms of Nygaard filtered absolute prismatic
cohomology. For such rings R, their absolute prismatic cohomology is a classical p-complete ring ∆R, which is
in fact a δ-ring equipped with a canonical prism structure (∆R, IR): this is the initial prism for R. In [BS22, §12],
Bhatt–Scholze construct the Nygaard filtration Fil•

Remark 2.1.5 (Functoriality and ´etale descent). Each of the assignments X 7→X?, for ? equal to ∆, N, or syn,
is functorial in the derived formal scheme X, and preserves products: That is, we have canonical isomorphisms
(X × Y)? ≃X? × Y?. Furthermore, if Y →X is an ´etale and faithfully flat map7, then Y? →X? is an ´etale cover
of derived formal stacks (see [GM24, Proposition 6.12.3]).
Definition 2.1.6. A map Y →X of derived p-adic formal schemes is quasisyntomic if it is p-completely flat
(that is, Y ⊗L Fp →X ⊗L Fp is flat), and if LY/X has p-complete Tor amplitude [−1, 0]: that is, LY/X/Lp has
Tor amplitude [−1, 0] as a quasi-coherent sheaf on Y ⊗L Fp. The map Y →X is a quasisyntomic cover if it is
quasisyntomic and Y ⊗L Fp →X ⊗L Fp is an fpqc cover.
Proposition 2.1.7. If Y →X is a quasisyntomic cover, then YN →XN is surjective in the p-completely flat
topology. In fact, if Y and X are the formal spectra of semiperfectoid rings, then YN →XN is faithfully flat.
Proof. See [GM24, Proposition 6.12.3 and Corollary 6.12.8].
□
Remark 2.1.8. If X = Spf R is affine and the structure map X →Spf(Zp) is quasisyntomic, there exists an affine
quasisyntomic cover Spf R∞= X∞→X such that R⊗Rm
∞

reduces questions about the stacks X? to ones about the corresponding stacks associated with (the formal spectra
of) qrsp and p-torsion free rings.

2.2. F-gauges and prismatic F-crystals. Let X be a derived p-adic formal scheme.
Definition 2.2.1 ((Prismatic) F-gauges). A vector bundle in prismatic F-gauges or simply vector bundle
F-gauge over X is a vector bundle over Xsyn. We will write Vect(Xsyn) for the category of such objects.8
Remark 2.2.2 (Prismatic F-crystals). Recall from [BS23] the notion of prismatic crystals and prismatic F-crystals
over X: These are objects over the absolute prismatic site X∆(shortened to R∆when X = Spf(R)). To begin, we
have the structure sheaf O∆and a generalized Cartier divisor I∆→O∆given by the assignment
(I∆→O∆): (A, I, Spf(A) →X) 7→(I →A).
A prismatic crystal (in vector bundles) over X is a vector bundle E over (X∆, O∆), and a prismatic F-crystal
is a pair (E, φE) where E is a prismatic crystal and
φE : φ∗E[1/I∆] ∼
−→E[1/I∆]
is an O∆[1/I∆]-linear isomorphism of sheaves on X∆. We write Vectφ(X∆, O∆) for the category of prismatic F-crystals.
Remark 2.2.3. Concretely, a prismatic crystal is an assignment
(A, I, Spf(A) →X) 7→E(A, I, Spf(A) →X)
with the value being a finite locally free A-module, and satisfying the usual crystal property:
For all maps
(A, I, Spf(A) →X) →(B, J, Spf(B) →X) in X∆, we have isomorphisms
E(A, I, Spf(A) →X) ⊗A B ∼
−→E(B, J, Spf(B) →X)
satisfying the expected compatibility relations. Endowing this with the structure of a prismatic F-crystal now
amounts to giving isomorphisms
E(A, I, R →A)[1/I] ⊗A,φ A ∼
−→E(A, I, R →A)[1/I]
that are compatible with the isomorphisms coming from the crystal property.
Remark 2.2.4 (F-gauges and prismatic F-crystals). By Remark 2.1.1, any vector bundle on X∆yields a prismatic
crystal over X. If the vector bundle arises from a vector bundle F-gauge via pullback along j∆: X∆→Xsyn, then E
has a canonical structure of a prismatic F-crystal over X; see [Bha, Remark 6.3.4] and [IKY23, Construction 1.21].
This gives rise to a natural functor Vect(Xsyn) →Vectφ(X∆, O∆).
Proposition 2.2.5. Suppose that X is p-quasisyntomic and flat over Zp. Then the natural functor
Vect(Xsyn) →Vectφ(X∆, O∆)
is fully faithful
Proof. See [GL25, Corollary 3.53].
□
2.3. Analytic prismatic F-crystals and crystalline local systems. We now recall some generalizations due
to Du–Liu–Moon–Shimizu [DLMS24] and Guo–Reinecke [GR24] of the classification of Bhatt–Scholze [BS23] of
crystalline Galois representations. For more details, see the discussion in [IKY24, §2.3]. In this subsection, K will
denote a complete mixed characteristic (0, p) discrete valuation field with perfect residue field.
Remark 2.3.1 (Analytic prismatic F-crystals). We will take Vectan,φ(X∆, O∆) to be the category of analytic
prismatic F-crystals defined in [GR24, §3]. Concretely, this amounts to giving essentially the same data as that
of a prismatic F-crystal, explained in Remark 2.2.3, except that E(A, I, Spf(A) →X) is now a vector bundle over
Spec(A)\V (p, I); [GR24, Lemma 3.4] ensures that this is a sensible notion.

Remark 2.3.5 (Crystalline local systems). Suppose that X is a base formal OK-scheme as defined in [IKY24, §1.1]
and the references therein. This implies in particular that R is p-quasisyntomic and p-torsion free. Then within

Zp (X) of crystalline Zp-local systems; see [DLMS24, §2.2], [GR24,

crystalline Qp-representation of the absolute Galois group of K. Recent work of Guo–Yang [GY24, Theorem 1.1]
shows that, when X is smooth over OK, a Zp-local system over X is crystalline if and only if its restriction over
every classical point of Xη is crystalline.

Zp (Xη).

Definition 2.3.7 (Local systems for smooth affine group schemes). Suppose that G is a smooth affine group scheme
over Zp and that X is a locally Noetherian scheme or adic space. A G(Zp)-local system over X is a G(Zp)-torsor
on the pro´etale site of X.10 We will write LocG(Zp)(X) for the groupoid of such local systems. If X is affine/affinoid
with global sections R, we will also write LocG(Zp)(R) instead. We will also use analogous notation for the variants
where G(Zp) is replaced by G(Z/pnZ). See [IKY24, §2.1] for a more detailed discussion.
Remark 2.3.8 (Tannakian perspective on local systems). Giving a G(Zp)-local system P over X is equivalent to
giving an exact symmetric monoidal functor V 7→(V )P from the category RepZp(G) of algebraic representations
G →GL(V ) defined over Zp to the category LocZp(X). See [IKY24, Propositions 2.3 and 2.8]. In fact, the cited
result shows that giving a G(Z/pnZ)-local system Pn over X is the same as giving an exact symmetric monoidal
functor RepZp(G) →LocZ/pnZ(X).
Definition 2.3.9 (Crystalline G(Zp)-local systems). Let X be a base formal OK-scheme. A G(Zp)-local system
P on Xη is crystalline if, for every representation V in RepZp(G), the local system (V )P is crystalline. Write
Loccrys
G(Zp)(Xη) for the full subcategory of LocG(Zp)(Xη) of crystalline G(Zp)-local systems.
Remark 2.3.10. By [IKY24, Proposition 2.20], to check that a G(Zp)-local system P is crystalline, it is sufficient
to check that (V )P is crystalline for a single faithful representation V .

bining Remark 2.3.8 with Theorem 2.3.6, we see that the category Loccrys
G(Zp)(Xη) is equivalent to the category of
exact symmetric monoidal functors
ω: RepZp(G) →Vectan,φ(X∆, O∆).
Corollary 2.3.12. Suppose that X is p-quasisyntomic and flat over Zp.
(1) There is a natural faithful functor
T´et : BG(Xsyn) →LocG(Zp)(Xη).
(2) If X is a base OK-formal scheme, then the functor is also full and takes its values in Loccrys
G(Zp)(Xη).
Proof. Under these hypotheses, Xsyn is a classical formal stack. Therefore, we find from [Lur18, Corollary 9.3.7.3]
that the groupoid (BG)(Xsyn) is equivalent to the groupoid of exact symmetric monoidal functors
RepZp(G) →Vect(Xsyn).
The corollary now follows from Theorem 2.3.6 and Remark 2.3.8.
□

3.2. Frames and windows. In [GM24, §5 and §10], we find a general discussion of frames and windows, which
allow us to give more concrete descriptions of the category of (G, µ)-apertures in certain situations. Here, we will
isolate a particular instance that will prove very useful to us.
Definition 3.2.1 (Breuil–Kisin frames). Let R be a p-complete ring. A Breuil–Kisin frame for R is a prism
A = (A, I′) such that A is p-complete and p-torsion free, I′ ⊂A is an ideal and R = A/I′.
Example 3.2.2 (Frames for base OK-algebras). Suppose that K is a complete discrete valuation field with perfect
residue field κ, and set K0 = W(κ)[1/p] ⊂K.
Fix a monic Eisenstein polynomial E(u) ∈W(κ)[u] such that
E(π) = 0 for some uniformizer π ∈OK. Suppose that R is a base OK-algebra in the sense of [IKY24, §1.1]: Then
R = R0 ⊗W (κ) OK where R0 is a flat W(κ)-algebra that admits a Frobenius lift and hence a δ-structure. The
prism
 SR, (E(u))

where SR = R0JuK is equipped with the δ-R0-algebra structure satisfying δ(u) = 0 is now a
Breuil–Kisin frame for R.
Example 3.2.3 (Frames for perfectoid rings). If R is a perfectoid ring, then ∆R = (∆R, Fil1
N ∆R) is a Breuil–Kisin
frame for R. Moreover, one has an identification ∆R ≃Ainf(R) under which Fil1
N ∆R corresponds to φ−1(ker θ)
where θ: Ainf(R) →R is Fontaine’s map.
Example 3.2.4 (Frames via Frobenius liftings). Suppose that R is a flat Zp-algebra equipped with a Frobenius
lift φ: R →R. We can then view

as a Breuil–Kisin frame R for R/pR.
Remark 3.2.5 (Liftings of ´etale covers). Suppose that A is a Breuil–Kisin frame for R. Then any p-completely
´etale map R →˜R lifts uniquely to a (p, I′)-completely ´etale map A →˜A of δ-rings, and ( ˜A, I′ ˜A) is a Breuil–Kisin
frame for ˜R; see [GM24, Proposition 5.4.23].
Assumption 3.2.6. For the remainder of this subsection, all our rings will be p-complete ˆO-algebras. Given such
an algebra R and a Breuil–Kisin frame A for R, the ˆO-algebra structure of R lifts uniquely to one on A.

Remark 3.6.16 (Work of Moonen and Shankar-Zhou). In [Moo04], Moonen defines the notion of a cascade and
shows that the deformation spaces of µ-ordinary formal O-modules have a natural cascade structure. This was
extended to Hodge type µ-ordinary deformation spaces by Shankar–Zhou [SZ21]. One can recover these structures
from the optic of Corollary 3.6.15 when (G, µ) is of Hodge type—that is, when we have a closed embedding of it into
(GLh, µd) for appropriately chosen h and d (see Example 3.1.6). However, an interesting point is that the cascade
structure does not appear to be canonical: It depends on the choice of representation into GLh.

4. Tate’s full faithfulness for apertures and its consequences
In this section, we prove a generalization Tate’s full faithfulness theorem for p-divisible groups to the context of
apertures. We also derive some corollaries to this that will be of importance for the main theorems of this paper.
4.1. Algebraization and Tate’s full faithfulness. Let O be a mixed characteristic (0, p) discrete valuation ring
with completion ˆO and fraction field E. We will now define an algebraic variant of BTG,µ
n
over O by gluing it to the
stack of G(Z/pnZ)-local systems over E, and we will use it to prove the aperture analogue of Tate’s full faithfulness
theorem for p-divisible groups by reducing it ultimately to Corollary 2.3.12.

The rest of this subsection and the next will be devoted to the proof of this theorem, with the proof of the
affineness of the diagonal completed in the next subsection. For now, we record an important consequence.
Definition 4.1.4 (η-normality). Suppose that X is a Deligne–Mumford stack over O. We will say that X is η-
normal17 if it admits an ´etale cover by affine schemes of the form Spec R with R both p-torsion free and integrally
closed in R[1/p]. Note that, if X is normal then it is automatically η-normal.
Theorem 4.1.5 (Tate full faithfulness for apertures). Let X be a Noetherian, η-normal Deligne–Mumford stack
over O with generic fiber X. Then the functor
T´et : BTG,µ,alg
∞

is fully faithful.

by Tate of his theorem for p-divisible groups [Tat67]. By Theorem 4.1.3, for any pair

Corollary 4.1.6. Suppose that X is a normal flat Noetherian Deligne-Mumford stack over O and X ′ →X is a
smooth cover. Then, for Q ∈LocG(Zp)(Xη), there exists a Q ∈BTG,µ,alg
∞
(X) with T´et(Q) isomorphic to Q if and
only if there is a Q′ ∈BTG,µ,alg
∞
(X ′) with T´et(Q′) isomorphic to Q|X ′[1/p].
Proof. The only if direction is obvious. For the if direction, since X ′ →X is smooth each k-fold self-fiber product
(X ′)×X k is still a normal flat Noetherian DM stack over O. Therefore, Theorem 4.1.5 tells us that the tautological
descent datum for Q|X ′η yields a descent datum for Q′, which is effective.
□
Remark 4.1.7. Combining the theorem with Theorem 2.4.2, we obtain a proof of Tate’s seminal full faithfulness
theorem for p-divisible groups [Tat67, Theorem 4]. Unwinding everything, one sees that the proof obtained here is
not very different from that of Tate’s, except that his final reduction is to the case where there exists a homomorphism
of p-divisible groups over a complete valuation ring inducing a given isomorphism of Tate modules. Since we do
not have any direct way of defining a map between apertures that is not an isomorphism, we end up appealing to
the faithfulness assertion of Theorem 2.3.6 instead.
Let us now begin preparations for the proof of Theorem 4.1.3.

5. Criteria for the existence of apertures
The purpose of this section is to develop criteria for proving the existence of apertures when G is reductive.
These are mainly of a ‘pointwise’ nature, and will be employed in the next section to prove the canonicity of
integral models. We will maintain the notation from Setup 3.1.1.
5.1. Apertures over p-completely smooth OK-algebras. Let K be a complete mixed characteristic (0, p)
discrete valuation with perfect residue field κ and absolute ramification index e, and fix a uniformizer π ∈K. Our
goal here is to characterize the image of the fully faithful ´etale realization functor for base formal OK-schemes. We
will assume that G is reductive.
Definition 5.1.1 (The type of a crystalline local system). Suppose that G is reductive and that we have Q ∈
Loccrys
G(Zp)(K). Then, for every representation V of G we obtain a filtered K-vector space Fil• DdR((V )Q). By [Kis10,
Lemma (1.4.5)], there exists a cocharacter of GK that splits these filtrations simultaneously for all V . In particular,
this gives us a well-defined geometric conjugacy class of cocharacters of G. This conjugacy class is the type of Q.
If λ is a representative of the conjugacy class defined over K, then we will abuse terminology and say that Q has
type λ. More generally, if X is a base formal OK-scheme, a crystalline G(Zp)-local systems Q over X is of type λ
if, for all V ∈RepQp(G), the filtration Fil•
Hdg DdR((V )Q) is, ´etale locally on Xη, split by the cocharacter λ.
Notation 5.1.2. For a cocharacter λ of GK, let Loccrys,λ
G(Zp)(K) be the groupoid of crystalline G(Zp)-local systems
of type λ on K. More generally, for any base formal OK-scheme X, we define Loccrys,µ
G(Zp)(Xη) to be the groupoid of
crystalline G(Zp)-local systems of type λ on Xη.
The two following results characterize the image of the fully faithful ´etale realization functor on BTG,µ
∞(R) in
some cases.

G(Zp)(K).

∞(X) →LocG(Zp)(Xη)
has essential image Loccrys,µ
G(Zp)(Xη).

fully faithful (e.g., see [Sta22, Tag 0EBJ]).

Remark 5.1.15 (The Hodge type case). If (G, µ) is of Hodge type, i.e., there exists a map (G, µ) →(GLh, µd) (see
Example 3.1.6) for some d such that the underlying map G →GLh is a closed immersion, then we can improve the
bound to p < e −1: Indeed, we can replace the use of the adjoint representation with the faithful representation
given by such a map, and this will have µ-weights in {0, 1}. When G = GLh this amounts to the assertion that
any crystalline local system over R[1/p] with Hodge–Tate weights in {0, 1} arises from a p-divisible group. This is
a result of Liu–Moon [LM20, Theorem 1.2].

Lemma 5.4.16. Suppose that p > 2 and that W is a smooth algebraic space over O, and suppose that f : V →Wη
is a finite Galois cover with abelian Galois group ∆satisfying the following property:
• There is a dense open subspace U of the special fiber Wk such that, for every w ∈U(κ), there exists a strongly
f-unramified lift ˜w ∈W(W(κ)) of w.
Then V extends to a Galois cover V →W.
Proof. Let F : V →W be the normalization of W in V . We want to show that F is finite ´etale. Since O →ˆO is
faithfully flat, F is finite ´etale if and only if its base change over ˆO is so. Moreover, V ⊗O ˆO is the normalization of
W ⊗O ˆO in V ⊗O[1/p] ˆO[1/p] as ˆO is a filtered colimit of smooth O-algebras [Sta22, Tag 07GB]. Therefore, without
loss of generality, we can assume that O = ˆO = W(κ) is p-adically complete and that k = κ is algebraically closed.
By d´evissage, we can reduce to the case where ∆= Z/ℓZ for some prime ℓ: This reduction only needs the
observation that, if we have a factorization V →W′ →W with the second map finite ´etale, then the preimage of
U in W′ satisfies the same properties as U but now with respect to V →W′.
Given our first hypothesis, it follows from Lemma 5.4.17 below that, for every generic point η of Vκ, the local
ring OV,η is a discrete valuation ring with maximal ideal generated by p.
By purity of the branch locus [Gro03, Corollaire 3.3], it suffices to know that, for any such η, lying above a generic
point η′ of Wκ, the map OW,η′ →OV,η of discrete valuation rings, both admitting p as a uniformizer, is ´etale. If
the finite extension of residue fields k(η)/k(η′) is separable, and in particular, if ℓ̸= p, then this is immediate.
Therefore, we can assume that ℓ= p and that the extension of residue fields is purely inseparable of degree p.
In this case, any generator of ∆will act as the identity on the residue field k(η). Since p > 2, this is impossible:
See for instance the proof of [Moo98, Proposition 3.22].
□

6. Integral canonical models: Definitions and first properties
In this section we apply the material established above to define and study the basic properties of integral
canonical models. Notably, we give a proof of Theorem A.
We will be following the conventions for Shimura varieties as laid out in [Del79].
Setup 6.1. Given a Shimura datum (G, X), for x ∈X, the conjugacy class {µx} of the associated Shimura
cocharacter µx26 is defined over the reflex field E ⊂C and is independent of the choice of x. We will denote this
E-rational class by {µ}. For a compact open subgroup K ⊂G(Af), let ShK be the canonical model over E for the
complex Shimura variety with complex points given by the orbifold
ShK(C) = G(Q)\X × G(Af)/K.
Note that ShK is a Deligne–Mumford stack over E that is a quasi-projective scheme when K is neat in the sense
of [Pin90, §0.6], and that in general ShK is the stack quotient of ShK′ by the finite group K/K′, for any choice of
neat compact open subgroup K′ ⊂K.
6.1. ´Etale realizations on Shimura varieties.
Definition 6.1.1 ([KSZ21, Definition 1.5.4 and Lemma 1.5.5]). A torus T over Q is cuspidal if it is isogenous to
a product of a Q-split torus and a Q-torus that is anisotropic over R. For a general Q-torus T, the anti-cuspidal
part Tac ⊂T is the minimal Q-subtorus such that T/Tac is cuspidal.
Remark 6.1.2. If T is a cuspidal torus over Q, then any subtorus is also cuspidal. This follows for instance from
characterization (v) in [KSZ21, Lemma 1.5.5].
Remark 6.1.3. The anti-cuspidal part is also characterized as the smallest subtorus of the maximal anisotropic
subtorus Ta ⊂T that contains the maximal R-split subtorus of Ta,R: in other words, it is the Q-Zariski closure in
T of the maximal R-split subtorus of Ta,R.
Definition 6.1.4 ([KSZ21, §1.5.6]). We define Gc = G/Z(G)ac, where Z(G) ⊂G is the center, and where Z(G)ac
is the anti-cuspidal part of the connected component Z(G)◦⊂Z(G): we refer to it as the cuspidal quotient of G.
Remark 6.1.5. As noted in the footnote on p. 34 of op. cit., this definition differs from the one used for instance
by Lovering in [Lov17b], since we are not assuming Milne’s axiom requiring that Z(G)◦split over a CM field. If
we made this assumption, then Z(G)ac is the maximal anisotropic subtorus of Z(G) that is R-split.
26For any representation W of GR, x yields a Hodge structure Wx on W and µx acts on W p,q
x
by z 7→z−p.

Remark 6.5.12 (Comparison with the definition of Milne–Moonen). In [Mil90], [Moo98], and [Kis10], one finds a
definition of integral canonical models that involves the inverse limit ShKp. An integral model SKp for ShKp has
the extension property if, for any regular and formally smooth OE,(v)-scheme S, any map Sη →ShKp of E-schemes
extends to a map S →SKp. If SKp is itself regular and formally smooth, this property characterizes it as an
integral canonical model in the sense of Milne.
Suppose that (G, G, X) admits limpid ICMs over OE,(v), and that the following additional condition holds:
• Given a discrete valuation field F over Ev and x ∈ShK(F), we have x ∈SK(OF ) precisely when EtK,ℓ|x
is unramified for all ℓ̸= p.29
We can now take the inverse limit over the resulting inverse system {SK} of finite ´etale maps to get a model
SKp for ShKp. We claim that SKp satisfies Milne’s extension property when p > 3.
First, we can assume that S = Spec R is affine. Now, for any mixed characteristic complete discrete valuation
field F over Ev with perfect residue field, and an x: Spec(F) →ShKp, the projection of xK to ShK lifts to an
OF -point of SK: Indeed, the existence of the lift x shows that, for all ℓ̸= p, the local system EtK,ℓ,xK is unramified
(in fact, trivial). Therefore, the desired assertion follows from our additional assumption above.
Next, by Popescu desingularization [Sta22, Tag 07GC], we can present R as a filtered colimit of smooth OE,(v)-
algebras implying S is an inverse limit of smooth affine OE,(v)-schemes Si. Given Sη →ShKp, the projection onto
ShK for any finite level K factors through Si,η for some i. The previous paragraph shows that the restriction of
EtK,p over Si,η is pointwise crystalline, and so Proposition 8.3.2 below now tells us that we have an extension
Si →SK, and this implies our claim.
Remark 6.5.13 (Comparison with the definition of Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman). Combining Remark 6.5.12 with
the discussion in [BST25a, §1.3] tells us that when p > 3, ICMs satisfying the additional assumption made above
also satisfy the canonicity property of Bakker–Shankar–Tsimerman.
7. Integral canonical models: Constructions and further properties
We will maintain the notation from above. In this section, we will prove our main theorems on the existence of
ICMs, as well as prove a few deeper properties of such models. All levels K ⊂G(Af) will be assume to be neat.
7.1. CM points and canonical models for tori.

subgroup KT ⊂T(Af), the tuple (T, {h}, KT ) yields a zero-dimensional Shimura variety ShKT over ET with
ShKT (C) ≃T(Q)\T(Af)/K.

Definition 7.3.2 (Quasi-isogenies in characteristic 0). For an E-algebra R and x, y ∈Sh(R), a quasi-isogeny
from x to y is an element g ∈G(Af) with x · g = y. We set QIsog(x, y) to be the set of such quasi-isogenies, and
shorten this to Aut◦(x) when x = y, in which case it is a subgroup of G(Af).

f).

x ∈ShK(F), and for all primes ℓ, the realization map Ix ⊗Qℓ→Iℓ,x is an isomorphism.

x ∈SK(κ). Then:
(1) There exists a reductive group Ix over Q with realization maps αℓ: Ix ⊗Q Qℓ→Iℓ,x for all ℓsuch that

Remark 7.3.16. When (G, X) is of pre-abelian type, Conjecture 7.3.14 can be deduced from and Faltings’s
isogeny theorem: Indeed, in the Siegel-type case the notion of quasi-isogeny reduces to the classical notion for
abelian varieties.
Conjecture 7.3.15, on the other hand, can be deduced from Kisin’s generalization of Tate’s
theorem [Kis17, Corollary (2.3.2)]. We will see in Theorem 7.4.22 that the latter also holds for all limpid ICMs
when x is a µ-ordinary point. This will be extended to a full proof (for finite κ) of the conjecture in [LM].
For later use, we record the following lemma.
Lemma 7.3.17 (Criterion for being a CM point). Let F be a field extension of E. Then the following are equivalent
for a point x ∈ShK(F):
(1) x is a CM point;
(2) after replacing F with a finite extension, the groups Ix and G have the same rank.
In fact, in this case, for every maximal torus T ⊂Ix, there exists a tuple (T, hT , i, g) as in Construction 7.1.8 such
that x is in the image of ShKT,g →ShK.
Proof. We only prove the more non-trivial implication, (2) implies (1), as this will be used below. In the process,
we will actually prove the more precise assertion following the equivalence.
Choose an embedding F ,→C and let xC ∈ShK(C) be the associated C-point. By lifting xC further to X ×G(Ap
f)
(see Remark 6.3.6), we can identify IxC with the commutant in G of the associated Hodge cocharacter h: S →GR.
By replacing F with a finite extension within C, we can ensure that the map Ix →IxC is an isomorphism.
Therefore, if T ⊂Ix is a maximal torus, this allows us to view TR as a maximal torus in GR, and in particular
identifies it with its own commutant in GR. This implies that h factors through TR, and hence shows that xC is a
CM point of ShK, as desired.
□

Shankar–Tsimerman [BST25a; BST25b].

unramified tuple (G, G, X, K) with a limpid ICM SK over OE,(v), and also maintain our assumption that G = Gc.

eventually empty, giving us the desired conclusion.

7.7. Shimura varieties of pre-abelian type.
Definition 7.7.1. A Shimura datum (G, X) is of Hodge type if G admits a faithful symplectic representation H
on which some (hence any) element of X induces a Hodge structure with weights (−1, 0), (0, −1). It is of abelian
type if there exists a Shimura datum (G1, X1) of Hodge type and a map Gder
1
→Gder
2
of derived groups inducing an
isomorphism on the corresponding adjoint Shimura data. It is of pre-abelian type if the adjoint Shimura datum
(Gad, Xad) is of abelian type.
Remark 7.7.2 (Classification of pre-abelian Shimura data). By the classification of Shimura data by Deligne [Del79,
§1.3], a Shimura datum (G, X) is of pre-abelian type if and only if each simple factor of the adjoint Shimura datum
(Gad, Xad) is classical of type A, B, C, DR, DH.
Note that a simple adjoint Shimura datum has underlying group of the form ResF/Q H where F is a totally real
field and H is an absolutely simple adjoint group over F. The meaning of type DR is that, for each embedding
τ : F →R, the group Hτ
defn
= H ⊗F,τ R is the adjoint quotient of an orthogonal group associated with a quadratic
form of signature either (2n, 2), (2n, n) or (2n + 2, 0). The meaning of type DH is instead that Hτ is the adjoint
quotient of O∗(2n), the group of isometries of a skew-Hermitian space over the ring of quaternions H. The Shimura
datum is of abelian type if the derived subgroup of every simple factor of GR of type DH is a quotient of SO∗(2n).
Example 7.7.3 (A non-abelian but pre-abelian Shimura datum). The basic example of a pre-abelian-type (but
not abelian-type) Shimura datum is obtained as follows. Take a totally real field F0 and a quaternion algebra B
over F0 such that for some distinguished archimedean place v0 of F0 we have Bv0 ≃H, and Bv ≃Mat2(R) for
archimedean places v ̸= v0. We then take a left B-module V of rank n ⩾4 with a Hermitian pairing which is
definitive at v ̸= v0 and signature (n, 0) at v0. Set Had to be PU(V, h) and Gad = ResF0/Q Had. Define h: S →Gad
R
to have trivial projection to each archimedean place v ̸= v0, be such that if W = V ⊗F0,v0 R then the projection
to v0 defines a Hodge structure of weight {(−1, 0), (0, −1)} where W (−1,0)
C
is the maximal isotropic subspace. This
defines a Shimura datum (Gad, Xad). Let H be the central cover of Had determined by the property that HC is
isomorphic to the GSpin cover of Had
C ≃PSO(2n). Then Xad admits a lift X to G = ResF0/Q H, and (G, X) is of
pre-abelian but not of abelian type.

Proposition 7.7.4 (Hodge type data satisfy WR). Suppose that (G, G, X) is of Hodge type. Then it satisfies
Assumption 7.6.7.
Proof. Suppose first that (G, X) is of Siegel type associated with a symplectic space V such that VQp admits a self-
dual lattice VZp stabilized by G. We begin by verifying that (G, G, X) admits limpid ICMs over Z(p) for any neat
level Kp ⊂G(Ap
f). The point is that ShK has an integral model SK over Z(p), given by a moduli space of polarized
abelian varieties with additional level structure. By Theorem 2.4.2, we have an F-gauge over SK associated with
the p-divisible group of the universal abelian scheme A →SK. We can actually view this as a (GL2g, µg)-aperture
over SK, where g is the relative dimension of A. Now, if rank VZp = 2g, Proposition 5.3.9 applies with
X = X ♯= SK; (G, µ) = (GSp2g, µg) ; (G♯, µ♯) = (GL2g, µg).
That c
SK,v →BTGL2g,µg
∞
is formally unramified follows from classical Serre–Tate theory and Theorem 2.4.2.
This tells us that SK satisfies the Serre–Tate property. The extension of the canonical ℓ-adic local systems over
SK for ℓ̸= p is obtained from the ℓ-adic Tate modules of A.
It remains to check the pointwise condition.
This comes down to the fact that, by a result of Coleman–
Iovita [CI99, Theorem 1], for an abelian variety A over a local field, crystallinity of the p-adic Tate module is
equivalent to saying that A has good reduction.
Now, let us proceed to the general Hodge type case, where we have a closed immersion from (G, X) into a Siegel
Shimura datum (G♯, X♯). This immediately implies that we have G = Gc. Moreover, by Zarhin’s trick, we can
assume that we actually have a closed immersion of unramified Shimura data
(G, G, X) →(G♯, G♯, X♯).
Therefore, the already considered case of Siegel type, combined with Theorem 7.2.1 tells us that, for any neat
Kp ⊂G(Ap
f) and any place v|p of E, ShK admits a limpid ICM over OE,(v).
A point x ∈SKp(κ) corresponds to a polarized abelian variety Ax over κ, equipped with an isomorphism

of quasi-projectivity along quotients by finite groups), and finally for all ICMs of pre-abelian type (by ascent of
quasi-projectivity along finite maps).

We can assume that the rank of G is at least 2, since otherwise the Shimura datum is of pre-abelian type and so is

crystalline if and only if N = 0. Therefore, x is potentially crystalline if and only if sp(x) ∈SK ⊗k ⊂S K ⊗k or,
equivalently, x ∈c
SK(F) = SK(OF ), as desired.

8. Applications and some complements
In this section, we collect some immediate applications and extensions of our theory of ICMs. In addition to
discussing cohomological applications and integral models at (some) parahoric levels, we prove Theorems H, J, and
I from the introduction. We freely use notation and terminology from §6–7.

which is the aim of this subsection.

Remark 8.1.4 (Functoriality properties of the de Jong extension property). Suppose that we have a map of

and OE2,(v2), respectively, with v1|v2 both lying over p. If G1 →G2 is a central cover or if G1 →G2 is a closed

From this, one checks that, if SK2 admits the de Jong extension property, then so does SK1.

Remark 8.1.7 (Extension property for pre-abelian type ICMs). The extension property holds for the Siegel
Shimura datum by results of de Jong [de 98, Corollaries 1.2 and 2.5]. Using Remarks 8.1.4 and 8.1.5, one sees that
it also holds for all ICMs for unramified Shimura tuples of pre-abelian type.
Remark 8.1.8 (Log de Rham extensions). Suppose that SK admits a log-smooth compactification S K such that,

8.3. Mapping properties for smooth inputs. In this subsection, we will give more concrete mapping properties
for ICMs in terms of pointwise criteria.
Definition 8.3.1 (Pointwise crystallinity). Suppose that we have an algebraic space X over OE,(v) equipped with
a map f : Xη →ShK. Let F/Ev be a finite extension and let x ∈X(OF ). Then, we say that f is crystalline at x
if EtK,p|x is crystalline. We say that f is pointwise crystalline if it is crystalline at all such x.
Proposition 8.3.2 (A pointwise mapping property for smooth inputs). Suppose that one of the following holds:
(a) p > 3;
(b) p > 2 and (G, X) is of pre-abelian type.
Suppose that SK is an ICM for ShK over OE,(v), and that X is a smooth OE,(v)-scheme with generic fiber X,
equipped with a map f : X →ShK. Then f extends to a map X →SK if and only if f is pointwise crystalline.
Proof. Suppose first that p > 3. Given Theorem 6.5.4, it is enough to know that EtK,p|X lifts to a (Gc, -µv)-aperture
over X. But this is immediate from Theorem 5.1.4 and Remark 6.4.4.
Next, suppose that (G, X) is of Hodge type and that p > 2. Then the same argument applies after taking
Remark 5.1.15 into account. The pre-abelian case can be deduced from this via standard arguments.
□
We have the following immediate corollary:
Corollary 8.3.3 (N´eronian property for proper models). Suppose that SK is proper over OE,(v), and with the
conditions from Proposition 8.3.2, SK is a N`eron model for ShK: In the notation of that proposition, every map
X →ShK extends to a map X →SK.
Remark 8.3.4 (Proper models of pre-abelian type). A necessary condition for properness of SK is that Gad be
anisotropic, since this is known to be equivalent to the generic fiber ShK being proper. With this condition in
hand, it follows from [Mad19, Theorem 1] that, when (G, X) is of pre-abelian type, and p > 2, the ICMs from
Theorem 7.7.6 are proper and so are N`eron models of their generic fibers.
Remark 8.3.5 (Sufficient to check crystallinity at one point). Suppose one knew that EtK,p is a pointwise semistable
local system on ShK: In the abelian type case, this follows from the structure of the boundary of the integral model
of a toroidal compactification; see [Wu25, Theorem 4.39] and earlier results in [Mad19]. This is also true for the
models in Theorem D as seen during its proof. We expect that this is true in general as well.
Under such a pointwise semistability condition, results of Guo–Yang [GY24, Theorem 1.1] and Diao–Yao [DY25b,
Theorem 1.1] allow us to considerably weaken the hypotheses of Proposition 8.3.2. Namely, in this case, statement
(1) from this proposition can be upgraded to only require f to be crystalline at x for some x ∈X(OF ) in every
connected component of X.
Remark 8.3.6. Suppose that p > 2 and (G, G, X, K) is an unramified Shimura tuple of pre-abelian type. We do not
know yet if EtK,p is semistable over ShK. However, since it suffices to know that the composition X →ShK →ShKad
extends to a map X →SKad, the strengthened mapping property from Remark 8.3.5 still holds for SK itself.

8.5. Integral canonical models for Shimura stacks. In this subsection, we will drop the assumption that K be
neat, but still assume that (G, G, X, K) is an unramified tuple. In this generality, ShK is a Deligne–Mumford stack
and not necessarily a scheme over E. However, we still have the map ShK →LocGc(Zp) classifying the Gc(Zp)-local
system EtK,p.
Definition 8.5.1 (Stacky integral canonical models). A stacky ICM for ShK over OE,(v) is a separated Deligne–
Mumford stack SK of finite type over OE,(v) with generic fiber ShK such that, for some neat compact open subgroup
K′ ⊂K with K′
p = Kp, normalization of SK in ShK′ is a limpid ICM for ShK′.
We can now collect the properties of stacky ICMs.
Remark 8.5.2 (Serre–Tate property for stacky ICMs). If SK is a stacky ICM for ShK, by Corollary 4.1.6, we see
that there is an extension SK →BTGc,-µv,alg
∞
of the classifying map for EtK,p, which is p-adically formally ´etale.
Remark 8.5.3 (Mapping property for stacky ICMs). Using ´etale descent, the mapping property from Theorem 6.5.4
holds verbatim for stacky ICMs, and one can even take Y to be an excellent separated η-normal Deligne–Mumford
stack over OE,(v), with the following caveat: One has to impose the condition that, for each ℓ̸= p, the restriction
of EtK,ℓover Yη extends to a Kc
ℓ-local system over Y represented by pro-algebraic spaces over OE,(v).
As a consequence, we get the following generalization of Corollaries 6.5.7 and 6.5.8:
Proposition 8.5.4 (Uniqueness and functoriality for stacky ICMs). Stacky ICMs are unique up to unique isomor-
phism and are functorial for maps of unramified tuples.
Proposition 8.5.5 (Existence of stacky ICMs). Suppose that for some neat open subgroup K′ ⊂K with K′ =
K
′,pKp, ShK′ admits a limpid ICM SK′ over OE,(v) in the sense of Definition 6.5.2. Then ShK admits a stacky
ICM over OE,(v). In particular, if one of the following conditions holds:
(1) p is large enough;
(2) (G, X) is of abelian type;
(3) p > 2 and (G, X) is of pre-abelian type.
Then ShK admits a stacky ICM over OE,(v).
Proof. The map ShK′ →ShK is a finite Galois cover of Deligne–Mumford stacks over E. Let ∆be its Galois group:
the action of ∆on ShK′ extends to an action on SK′ by Theorem 6.5.4 and the fact that EtK′,p is ∆-equivariant.
Therefore, we can set SK = [SK′/∆]: This is a Deligne–Mumford stack over OE,(v), and is clearly a stacky ICM
for ShK by construction.
□

8.6. Integral canonical models over global rings of integers.
Setup 8.6.1. We drop our fixed prime p. Instead, take an open subgroup K ⊂G(Af) and let ΞK be the set of
primes ℓwhere K ∩G(Qℓ) is not hyperspecial. For S ⊇ΞK a set of primes, there exists a reductive group scheme
GS over Z[S−1] with GS(Zp) = Kp for p /∈S. Write GS,c for the quotient of GS by the Zariski closure of Z(G)ac.
Remark 8.6.2. If G admits a reductive model GZ over Z, and we take K = GZ(bZ), then ΞK is empty. For instance
if G = GSp2g,Q, then we can take GZ = GSp2g,Z. In this case, the associated Shimura stack ShK is the moduli stack
Ag,Q over Q of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g.
Definition 8.6.3 (Global integral canonical models). Let S ⊇ΞK be a set of primes. An integral model SK for
ShK over OE[S−1] is a (stacky) limpid integral canonical model for ShK if, for every p /∈S and every place
v|p of E, SK ⊗OE OE,(v) is a (stacky) limpid ICM for ShK over OE,(v).
Let us list some results concerning these global ICMs that follow easily from the local versions. The version of
these results for global stacky ICMs are formulated and proven similarly.
Proposition 8.6.4 (Mapping property for global ICMs). Let Y be an excellent separated normal Deligne–Mumford
stack flat over OE[S−1] with generic fiber Y over E. Suppose that we are given a map f : Y →ShK with the
following properties:
(1) For every prime p /∈S and every v|p, the map Y →LocGS,c(Zp) classifying EtK,p extends to a map
Y ⊗OE OE,(v) →BT
GS,c

.

ℓ-local system EtK,ℓ|Y extends to a Kc
ℓ-local system on Y[ℓ−1] represented by pro-
algebraic spaces over OE,(v).
Then f extends to a map Y →SK.
Proposition 8.6.5 (Uniqueness and functoriality for global ICMs). An ICM SK over OE[S−1] is determined
uniquely up to unique isomorphism. In fact, for a map f : (G1, X1) →(G2, X2) of Shimura data with f(K1) ⊂K2,
and ICMs SKi over OEi[S−1], the canonical map ShK1 →ShK2 ⊗E2E1 extends to a map
SK1 →SK2 ⊗OE2 OE1.
Theorem 8.6.6 (Existence of global ICMs). The following statements hold.

K ] (resp.
over OE[2−1Ξ−1

(2) In general, ShK admits a limpid ICM over OE[S−1] for some large enough set of primes S containing ΞK.
Remark 8.6.7 (Global ICMs of Hodge type). Suppose that (G, X) is of Hodge type and that we have an embedding
(G, X) ,→(GSp(V ), S±(V )) into a Siegel Shimura datum associated with a symplectic space V over Q. Then the
proof of Theorem C shows that the global (stacky) ICM SK for ShK is obtained by taking the normalization of a
moduli stack of polarized abelian varieties over OE[S−1] in ShK. In particular, Ag, the stack of principally polarized
abelian varieties, is a global stacky ICM for its generic fiber.
Remark 8.6.8 (Global ICMs of abelian type). For Shimura varieties of abelian type, a version of statement (1) in
the theorem above is due to Lovering [Lov17b]. His proof amounts to a careful finite-level analysis of a combination
of Deligne’s quotient construction from [Del79] with Kisin’s twisting construction from [Kis10], whereas the result
here is an immediate consequence of our local characterization of ICMs at finite level.
Remark 8.6.9 (Automorphic bundles over global ICMs). Let GrX be the projective homogeneous G-equivariant
variety over E parameterizing parabolic subgroups of G of type {-µ}. This has a natural integral model GrS
X over
OE[S−1] defined in the same fashion using parabolic subgroups of GS. The Hodge-filtered de Rham realizations
from Construction 6.4.5 for varying places v|p with p /∈S can be glued together to get a canonical map
SK →GrS
X/GS.
As in Remark 6.4.8, for any finite extension F/E and any parabolic subgroup P ⊂GS
OF of type {-µ}, a functor from
representations of P to vector bundles over SK ⊗OE OF . In the abelian-type case, this recovers Lovering [Lov17b].

CANONICITY OF INTEGRAL MODELS OF SHIMURA VARIETIES

8.7. Integral canonical models at parahoric level. Here we explain that any unramified Shimura datum
(G, G, X) which admits an ´etale integral canonical model automatically admits a Pappas–Rapoport integral canon-
ical model as studied in [PR24]. We then apply results of Takaya from [Tak25] to obtain Pappas–Rapoport integral
canonical models for subhyperspecial (e.g., Iwahori) level. Finally, we give an analogue of the mapping property
from Theorem 6.5.4 in this parahoric situation.
Notation 8.7.1. In the following we denote by Perf A, for a p-adically complete ring A, the category of charac-
teristic p perfectoid spaces S equipped with a map S →Spd(A). For any other undefined piece of notation or
terminology, we direct the reader to the comprehensive discussion in [PR24, §2-3].
Definition 8.7.2 (Tame local Shimura datum). A tame local Shimura datum is a triple (G, b, {µ}) where G is
a parahoric group Zp-scheme with generic fiber G, {µ} is a conjugacy class of minuscule cocharacters of GQp, and
b is an element of G(˘Qp) inducing an element of B(G, -µ).
Definition 8.7.3 (Local Shimura variety). Given a tame local Shimura datum (G, b, {µ}) with reflex field F, we
obtain a presheaf
Mint
G,b,{µ} : Perf O ˘
F →Set,
assigning to S →Spd(O ˘
F ) the set of isomorphism classes of tuples (S♯, P, ϕP, ir), where:
• S♯is the untilt of S over O ˘
E associated with S →Spd(O ˘
F ),
• (P, ϕP) is a G-shtuka on S with one leg along S♯bounded by {µ} (see [PR24, Definition 2.4.3]),
• and ir is a framing (see loc. cit.).
By [SW20, §25.1], the presheaf Mint
G,b,{µ} is a small v-sheaf (in the sense of [Sch17, Definition 12.1]), which is called
the integral local Shimura variety associated with the tame local Shimura datum (G, b, {µ}).
Definition 8.7.4 (Tame Shimura datum/tuple). A tame Shimura datum is a triple (G, G, X) where (G, X) is
a Shimura datum and G is a parahoric model of GQp. A tame Shimura tuple is a quadruple (G, G, X, K) where
(G, G, X) is a tame Shimura datum and K ⊂G(Af) is a compact open subgroup with K = KpKp and where
Kp = G(Zp). We say (G, G, X, K) is neat if K is.
Remark 8.7.5. Fix a neat tame Shimura tuple (G, G, X, K) with reflex field E and a place v of E of lying over p.
In the following we shall abusively write ShK for ShK(G, X)Ev. We will write {µv} for the conjugacy class of GQp
determined by the Hodge cocharacter of (G, X).
As in Construction 6.1.7 for one may build pro´etale Gc(Zp)-torsor EtK,p on ShK. Here Gc is the parahoric model
of Gc induced by the parahoric model G of G (e.g., see [DY25a, §4.1]). As in the reductive case, we treat {µv} also
as a conjugacy class of cocharacters of Gc

Definition 8.7.8 (Pappas–Rapoport integral models). Fix a tame Shimura datum (G, G, X). Then, a system {SK}
of normal flat OEv-models of ShK, as one ranges over all sufficiently small neat tame Shimura tuples (G, G, X, K),
is a Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical model for (G, G, X) if it satisfies conditions (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv)
of [DY25a, Definition 4.3].
A Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical model is crystalline if, for any K, and for any mixed characteristic
complete discrete valuation field F over Ev with perfect residue field, the following are equivalent for x ∈ShK(F):
(1) x ∈SK(OF );
(2) EtK,p|x is crystalline;
(3) EtK,p|x is potentially crystalline.
Proposition 8.7.9. Suppose that (G, G, X) is an unramified Shimura datum admitting ICMs SK for all sufficiently
small neat Kp ⊂G(Ap
f). Suppose also that these integral models satisfy the additional pointwise condition from
Remark 6.5.12. Then the collection {SK} is a crystalline Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical model for (G, G, X).
Proof. The crystallinity condition is immediate from our definition of ICMs, so it is enough to check the condi-
tions from [DY25a, Definition 4.3] for Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical models. Conditions (i) and (ii) both
follow from Remark 6.5.12.
For condition (iii), observe each SK carries a prismatic F-crystal in the sense of
[IKY24, Definition 3.18] with de Rham local system EtK,p. Therefore, applying the shtuka realization functor from
[IKY24, Construction 3.19] to this prismatic F-crystal yields a shtuka PK on S ♢/
K . That PK models PK,E is
clear as (PK)E is (by definition) Usht(EtK,p) = PK,E. To check (iv), we may use the formal ´etaleness of the
syntomic realization ϖ and [IKY23, Proposition 3.32] (see also [GM24, §10.2]) to reduce ourselves to constructing
an isomorphism
Θx :

where the target is as in loc. cit., and with the property that pullback of PK on the target is the universal Gc-shtuka
on the source. But this is the content of [Ito25, Theorem 5.3.5].
□
Definition 8.7.10 (Subhyperspecial parahoric). Let F/Qp be a finite extension and H a reductive group over F.
A subgroup of H(F) is subhyperspecial parahoric if it is a parahoric subgroup in the sense of [KP23, Definition
4.1.4] contained in H(OF ) for a reductive OF -model H of H.
Remark 8.7.11 (Explicit description of subhyperspecial parahorics). For a reductive model H of H over OF , the
subhyperspecial parahorics contained in H(OF ) are precisely those of the form red−1(P(k)) where k is the residue
field of F, P ⊂Hk is a parabolic subgroup, and
red: H(OF ) →H(k)
is the reduction map. Equivalently, the subhyperspecial parahoric subgroups of H(K) are H′(OK) for a parahoric
group OK-scheme H′ (see [DY25a, Definition 2.6]) obtained as the dilatation (see [KP23, §A.5]) of a reductive
OF -model H along a parabolic subgroup P ⊂Hk. We call these OK-models H′ of H parabolic dilatations of H.
We will just call H′ a parabolic dilatation if H is implicit.
Example 8.7.12 (Iwahoris are subhyperspecial). Considering red−1(B(k)) for B ⊂Hk a Borel subgroup we obtain
the (or more precisely a) Iwahori subgroup of H(F) as in [KP23, Definition 4.1.3].
Proposition 8.7.13. Let (G, G, X) be an unramified Shimura datum satisfying (SV5)34 and admitting ICMs
satisfying the condition from Remark 6.5.12. Then, for any parabolic dilatation G′ of G, the tame Shimura datum
(G, G′, X) admits a crystalline Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical model.
Proof. This is immediate from Proposition 8.7.9 and [Tak25, Theorem 6.20].
□
Corollary 8.7.14. Suppose that (G, G′, X) is a tame parahoric Shimura datum with G′ a parabolic dilatation.
Suppose further that either:
34Recall that this means that Z(G)(Q) is discrete in Z(G)(Af). This, in particular, implies that G = Gc.

(1) (G, X) is of abelian-type;
(2) (G, X) is of pre-abelian type and p > 2;
(3) p is sufficiently large.
Then, (G, G′, X) admits a crystalline Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical model.
Remark 8.7.15 (Agreement with Kisin–Pappas–Zhou). When (G, G′, X) is of abelian type and p > 3 the models
constructed by Corollary 8.7.14 agree with the Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models constructed in [KP18] and [KPZ24].
Indeed, this follows from the unicity of Pappas–Rapoport integral canonical models (see [PR24, Theorem 4.3.1])
and the results of [DY25a], building on previous work from [PR24], [DvHKZ25], and [Dan25].
Definition 8.7.16 (Moduli stack of (G, µ)-shtukas). Let notation be as in Definition 8.7.3. Denote by ShtG,{µ} the
v-stack on Perf OF sending S →Spd(OF ) to the groupoid of G-shtukas over S with leg along S♯bounded by {µ}.
The proof of the following result is along the same lines as that of Theorem 6.5.4. One has to replace the use of
Theorem 4.1.5 with [PR24, Theorem 2.7.7], and also appeal to [SW20, Proposition 18.4.1].
Theorem 8.7.17. Let (G, G, X) be a tame Shimura datum admitting a crystalline Pappas–Rapoport integral canoni-
cal model {SK} over OEv. Suppose that X is an η-normal algebraic space over OEv. Then a map Xη →ShK extends
to a map X →SK if and only if the composition X ♢
η →Sh♢
K →ShtG,{-µv} extends to a map X ♢/ →ShtG,{-µv}.
